    So the 18-300 mm question is: Having eliminated 1st Lens Group alignment from consideration, what is the most likely part to fail from a minor traumatic event like whacking the filter ring or just from wear and tear? If the VR armature or its lens popped off the suspension or got stuck to one side, VR would not work at all. But VR works great. If any other lens group came off its tracks, there would be obvious problems with zoom or focus. Only if a lens element or elements) came unglued within their housing(s) could there be optical problems but no mechanical problems. If anyone actually reads this stuff ;-) and has a similar experience, knows the answer, or even has a wild guess, please contact me via the Sci.Electronics.Repair FAQ Email Links Page. For now, I'm not intending to go any further inside.

    Of note is that even perfect specimens of the 18-300mm f/3.5-5.6 lens have significant distortion and chromatic aberration of varying degrees over various portions of the zoom and focus range. Achieving decent performance over a greater than 16:1 zoom range is challening. But modern DSLRs digitally process the images to largely correct both geometric distortion and color fringing using lens-specific data stored in memory (part of the camera firmeware). So the end-user may never see most of these. unless they are obsessive-compulsive and search for defects like that. It may be surprising how mediocre its performance really is when viewing the raw image data. And this lens has 19 lens elements. That's 38 anti-reflection-coated surfaces the light must traverse, which will have some effect on general sharpness, flare, etc. (But none of that can be corrected in-camera.)

    Being slightly disappointed with the performance, I even contacted Nikon via their Website with the serial number to see if they would tell me anything about that specific lens but of course that was hopeless. Their reply was that they don't have any information and suggested that I send it in for service. ;( ;-)

    And a trivial triviality common to both (and I assume to all of these): The zoom marking for 200 mm seems to correspond more to somewhere between 225 and 250 mm, NOT 200 mm based on the image size compared to the 300 mm position. I've not seen any mention of that in the reviews of this lens or on Nikon lens forums.

    How far it goes will depend on when - if ever - the cause of the lousy pictures can be found. My guess is a lens group that has been knocked out of position, or perhaps the VR Lens Group even though VR appears to work well. If one of the springs in the VR assembly popped off, VR might still appear to work.

    For the start of a dissection, see Nikon AF-S DX Nikkor 18-300mm f 3.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album. This will be guided by the repair manual but there will be significant deviations. ;-)

    And yes, you aren't hallucinating. There are closeups of a piece of cloth and shots of an ancient digital computer light and switch panel amongst the dissection photos. The reason for these is explained later below. ;-)

    Here are some more comments on the dissection photos:

    • The first four are stock photos showing this lens by itself and in comparison with the other large-zoom range lens and the AF-S DX 18-55mm f/1:3.5G VR II kit lens.

    • The next two show the optical design of the lens and the approximate position of the lens groups at various zoom settings. These were measured so they should be fairly accurate. (If interested, load the image at full resolution by using this filename: AF-S-DX-18-300mm-f3.5-5.6G-VR-Lens_Group_Positions.jpg in the same folder. Then the scale is 100 pixels/inch.)

    • The photos bookended by the Nikon boxes are of a pristine sample of this lens.

    • After that are photos of a test chart showing how dreadful the defective lens really is.

    • Next are photos of that lens as the front is discombobulated for the dissection. Every attempt has been made to do this in a reversible manner. That may be asking a lot but so far the only casualties are the Large Light Shield, which tore upon removal, and the locations of the 3 partial shims under the 1st Lens Cluster. But it was never certain that they were in the correct positions at all when I acquired the lens, and then after I did some initial exploration.

    • The next set of photos show images taken with this lens of a piece of cloth and 35 mm Kodachrome slide positioned at the magic "sweet" spot described below at various apertures, and then a comparison of a shot of the same slide taken using a AF-S DX Micro Nikkor 40mm f/1:2.8 Macro lens with home-built slide copying adapter. More on these below.

    • The next set of photos of the computer console were made with the same slide adapter as for the full frame shot using the Macro lens, but used with this lens instead. In another coincidence, the coverage was nearly the same. Focus was carefully adjusted at the center. As can be seen, the quality is totally dreadful - much more so than the previous shots - and a bit inconslusive. However, perhaps of note is that the fuse holders in the lower part of the console remain is focus relative to the upper left even at f/1:5.6. In fact, focus at the center appears worse, which is strange. But, the sharpness is actually better at f/16 than f/25. There is little or no pincusion distortion. ;-) A better test slide is needed.

      For the record, in the "well that's interesting but probably of little value for anything department", some typical Field of Views (FoVs) are very approximately: 

         Zoom    Location      FoV       Notes
 -----------------------------------------
   18 mm     7.0"    2.0" x 1.33"
   20 mm     6.0"    1.5" x 1.00"   FX 
   30 mm     2.5"    1.0" x 0.67"   DX
   70 mm     0.0"    0.5" x 0.33"
   90 mm    -0.4"    0.4" x 0.27"

      where "Location" is the distance from the edge of the Filter Ring. Since the optical elements comprising the AP/VR assembly can be modeled as a single convex lens, this is just basic ray optics, more-or-less, with worse performance than a bog standard pin-hole. ;-)

    • The photo of the red blob was made by passing the beam of a HeNe laser expanded to approximately 12 mm through the lens to a screen at a distance of around a meter. The rings are in the original beam from an HP 5517 laser tube assembly. The ripples are from dust somewhere in the optical path. And the ding on the right it a defect in the beam expander output aperture.

      The key takeaway from the shot is that the ring pattern is fairly symmetric. And it doesn't change much shifting the lens angle, moving it from side-to-side, or flipping it end-for-end. In addition, the beam focuses to a tiny dot just after the lens, which is also symmetric in front and behind that point. Taken together, the results are really strange and pretty much eliminate this last remaining lens cluster as the cause of the lousy lens performance. But given everything else, there is no other possibility!

    • The last 11 shots show the reassembled lens and sets of closeup photos at a Zoom of 300mm and apertures of f/5.6, f/8, f/16, and f/32 of a cheap compass and antique pocket watch movement.

    Now for more details.

    The first part of the dissection dealt with removing the Front (1st) and Middle 1 (2nd) lens clusters. They appear pristine and there is nothing obivous inspecting beyond those from the front. There is evidence that 2nd Lens Cluster had been removed previously but I can't rule out that being my doing.

    ThAt state of disassembly offered a possible way to confirm that the problem was still present and associated with the back of the lens. It turns out that with the 1st and 2nd Lens Clusters removed and Zoom set at 18 mm, an "object" against the front of the Zoom Barrel (just behind the edge of the Filter Ring) will be in sharp focus in the camera. Whether there is some cosmic significance to this with respect to the lens design is not known but it is a remarkable coincidence. ;-) The area covered is around 1.0 x 0.6 inches, close to the APS-C DX format. So a 35 mm slide (remember those?) or 35 mm negative, a textured surface, or something similar can be secured there with masking tape. If it is too close, Zoom can be used for manual focus since it moves that surface without affecting anything else significantly over the small range required. (Focus would not do anything without the 2nd Lens Cluster installed.) There is a small amount of pincushion distortion but one can't have everything. ;-) And a photo editor like Evoto will easily correct that. Preliminary results are encouraging in confirming the problem is with the back section of the lens:

    The photos titled: AF-S-DX-18-300mm-f3.5-5.6G-ED-VR_No_1st_2nd_Lens_Clusters_Cloth1_f5.6, f8, f16, and f29 were taken using a D5600 of a piece of fine thread cloth positioned at the lens's sweet spot using similar aperture settings to the test chart photos. The same asymmetric fuzziness appears to be present.

    The photos titled: AF-S-DX-18-300mm-f3.5-5.6G-ED-VR_No_1st_2nd_Lens_Clusters_Univac_1830_Console_Slide1_f5.6, f8, f16, and f22 are of a 35 mm Kodachrome slide originally taken in the year 1978 using a Nikon Photomic FTN body with f/1:1.4 50 mm lens of the operator's console of a Univac 1830 (CP-823/U) avionics computer prototype from the early 1960s. (For some obscure reason the D5600 refused to stop down beyond f22 for these shots even though it was set to Manual exposure.)

    (For the techno-dweeb types and having nothing to do with this lens, the Univac 1830 was a 30 bit computer that had a whopping 32,768 words of core memory and utilized flat-pack SSI integrated circuits for most of its logic. Wikipedia CP-823/U has more including a photo of the nine massive electronics boxes that comprised the actual computer, all of which I had at one point along with two or three cartons of garden-hose diameter cables. The photos of the console there are ones I contributed. The first one is derived from a scan of the exact same slide used for this dissection. These may be the only color photographs of the 1830 in existence today.)

    For comparison, the photo titled: AF-S-DX-18-300mm-f3.5-5.6G-ED-VR_Univac_1830_Slide_with_Macro_Lens1 was taken of the same slide using an AF-S DX Micro Nikkor 40mm f/1:2.8 Macro lens with the slide mounted approximately 2.5 inches in front of the lens with a light diffuser and cropped to show the entire 35 mm frame. This is a fairly accurate comparison of the area of the original slide and the smaller coverage of the hacked 18-300mm lens along with its pincushion distortion.

    Next was the dissection of the back of the lens. The Back Lens Cluster also appears to be in perfect condition - at least until I disassembled the two sections with the 12th and 13th Lens Clusters and 14th Lens Cluster, which is something that should not be done without the proper tools and test procedures to realign them!)

    But here again, the lens cooperated in that with *only* the Aperture/VR assembly still present, the lens would focus as a macro lens over a range of a few inches.

    And the shot of the laser spot seems to rule out the AP/VR Lens Cluster as the cause of the problem. Which means the lens should have performed flawlessly. Too bad that it didn't understand that. ;-)

    I decided to put it back together. It was straightforward since nothing except the one Light Shield was damaged Using the same laser, it should be possible to align and reassemble the Back Lens Cluster. It's just a matter of screwing the 2nd Lens Cluster (Focus) back in. And while the location of the shims for the 1st Lens Cluster are suspect, any effects from them being arrange incorrectly should be small.

    Everything has been reinstalled except for the label, light shields, front lens cluster shims, and rubber boot. The back lens cluster lens groups were centered as best as possible without going to the extreme a laser. ;-) The last photo in the Web Album shows the reassembled lens. The lens still takes truly lousy pictures. It's hard to tell if they are worse than before since they were already terrible. And it's also hard to tell if the character of the faults has changed as a result of alignment or anything else, but it's not dramatically worse. Probably no more than additional flare resulting from the lack of the light shields.

    And that, as they say, is that. ;-) Deeper dissection of this specific lens is probably not going to happen. However, if someone has a certifiably dead sample of the AF-S DX 18-300mm f/1:3.5-5.6G ED VR lens they would be willing to donate to the cause, please contact me via the Sci.Electronics.Repair FAQ Email Links Page.

    Nikon AF-S DX Nikkor 18-300mm f/3.5-6.3G ED VR Zoom Lens

    This is the newer and lighter version of 1the 18-300 mm lens but it has lost a fraction of a stop at the high end - 6.3 versus 5.6 which is considered mostly irrelevant with modern DSLRs. Its weight is 19.4 oz. (550 g), about 2/3rds that of the 18-300 mm f/3.5-5.6, which is actually slightly less than that of the 18-200 mm VR II lens - 19.8 oz. (560 g). It must use a lot more plastic! :( ;-)

    I am not aware of any on-line service manual for this lens, which appears to differ significantly compared to the previous 3.5-5.6 version (above, for which there is a service manual).

    • ID #1 (SN: US6001638) This is a certifiably broken sample and it was used for the first block of dissection photos, below. But not inside. The main issue is that VR does not do anything useful. That could be as simple as a bad switch or wire that came off. This is a well used lens but focus (A and M) are both functional. And though zoom is a bit hard to move at times, nothing appears really damaged. But there is a serious nick in the lens hood mount ring (visible in a few of the photos) so it has experienced some trauma.

      See the Nikon AF-S DX Nikkor 18-300mm f/3.5-6.3G VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album.

      This lens does take photos as long as one is careful to avoid camera shake, so reducing it to parts may not happen for a while. But the back was opened to check for obvious problems. The flex cables were all secure and the VR switch was tested so an easy fix is unlikely. However, if removing the bayonet and related parts, make sure to record the precise location of the brass shims - which I of course neglected to do initially. This specific sample has 2 and they are not identical so the orientation and also which one is on top may matter, though it's not clear why. And when reinstalling the aperture tang, take care that it goes into the correct location in the aperture actuator. Unlike many other lenses, it's a rectangular hole on this one, not just cup or U-shaped lever. It can slip in outside the hole and while it may appear to work when first installed, when the Inner Ring is put in place, it will jam. Other than that, reassembly is quite straightforward. There are only 4 types of screws and where they go should be intuitively obvious. ;-) But some are quite small so losing them is too easy and would be bad. ;-( And don't overtighten them!

      As they say: "If it ain't broke, don't fix it.". After going inside, two things changed: First, the image through the viewfinder "jerks" initially when the camera is turned on and the shutter is pressed, but only when VR is on. However, VR still doesn't do anything else. This indicates that VR is being powered but is jamming to one side unable to initialize. I don't think VR did anything when first testing the lens. So perhaps that's an improvement. ;-) Second, autofocus stopped working reliably. I could have sworn it wasn't like that initially. Sometimes it would lock but usually it would either get stuck at one end of its range, or continue hunting back and forth around the optimal focus unless very close, in which case it might lock. So the back parts were removed again and it was discovered that one of the brushes in the Focus Encoder wasn't contacting the encoder strip. I have no idea how that could have occurred during the excursion inside as it's safely recessed. But once the brush was "adjusted", autofocus is back in shape. The microbrain had no idea where focus was at any give time so it didn't know what to do. Poor thing. ;-)

      I also reseated all the internal flex cables at the same time as dealing with the focus issue but that didn't make any positive difference to VR.

      However, now the lens produces 4-1/2 "clunks" about 1/2 second apart when the camera is powered up and sometimes when it's powered down regardless of whether VR is on, independent of zoom and focus settings, but only with autofocus enabled. They sound like they are coming from the vicinity of the VR assembly but don't appear to do anything to the image in the viewfinder. The count of 4 is digitally precise and the behavior is totally repeatable. Go figure.

      Having said all that, the lens takes great photos and therefore I have no plans to go inside again. If only it could be filled with helium so it would weigh less (though it is actually a tad lighter than the 18-200 mm VR lens). And the older version was even heavier so see the info on it in the previous section, above. ;-)

      Of course all great plans need modification at some point. With the acquisition of ID #2, the 1st Lens Cluster was used for a transplant...

    • ID #2 (SN: US6063861) Unfortunately, in principle this could be more involved than just swapping the optic though how true that may be in practice is still not known. For the 18-55mm f/1:3.5-6.5 lens, there may be one or more full spacer rings and many small shims at 120 degree locations. It is believed that these are associated primarily with the 1st Lens Cluster, and not the overall lens since the molds and machining should be very precise. In that case, the orientation of the 1st Lens Cluster is also critical and must be retained, so label it relaive to the main index mark! But it is also possible that they compensate for variations elsewhere. (I should have first simply swapped the 1st Lens Cluster without messing with the spacers and shims at all to see how well that worked. It might have been fine. Oh well.) Loosing track of where they came from or loosing them entirely is all too easy. Thicknesses were measured to be approximately 0.001" for the stainless steel shims, 0.002" for the copper shims, and 0.004" for the brass shims. (That may also be true of the spacers but I didn't check their thicknesses.) So it is a binary 1,2,4 relationship to simplify selecting a specific thickness. The first rule is to keep track of exactly where they were originally located. That is particularly challenging given that the shims for this lens are very small and tend to jump out of position and fly away. The spacer rings which tend to be thicker (but not always) and thicker shims would be most critical but the thinner shims could possibly impact edge sharpness for low f-number shots. I think the shims were restored in the correct locations but it's hard to tell. Even picking them up and positioning them is virtually impossible. In the end, a piece of double-sticky tape wrapped around a small screwdriver was used to pick them up and place them on the locating pegs while then holding them in place with a toothpick. Fingers and tweezers were useless.

      If just swapping the 1st Lens Cluster would have been sufficient, that is possible without disturbing the spacers or shims: Remove ONLY the three 1st Lens Cluster mounting screws and lift it off taking care that if any shims come away with it, they are replaced in the same location. DO NOT remove the the Filter Ring since it holds the spacers in place.

      The spacer ring(s) and the thickness common to all the shims will slightly affect the closest focus distance and possibly being even able to focus at infinity if too much thickness is present. So, in conjunction with the spacer ring(s) at the back of the lens, they may be used to tune close focus distance. But exactly how much the slight tilt imparted by the unequal shims really matters is questionable give the HUGE free play present in most of these lenses - which easily exceeds any shim thickness differences.

      Regardless, after reassembly the lens appears to work perfectly and the photos it takes are excellent. The focus range and autofocus go a bit closer than the spec value by a couple inches but includes infinity at all zoom settings so that may be within acceptable limits. I have not checked the EXIF metadata. There are no obvious sharpness issues, asymmetric or otherwise.

      In the future, I would suggest measuring the close focus distance at maximum zoom before changing anything and then swap only the 1st Lens Cluster. Re-check performance, particularly the close focus distance. If that hasn't changed significantly (say less than 1 inch for this model lens) and the lens otherwise seems acceptable, don't worry, be happy. ;-) The EXIF metadata values may be not be precise, but they are not precise to begin with, depending on the focus encoder values. I kind of doubt that the focus servo tuning will be affected enough to matter, if at all. Any edge sharpness issues may only be detectable by taking high resolution photos of a resolution chart on a test stand and not in real life. And maybe not even then.

    Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G ED Zoom Lens

    There appear to be no on-line repair manuals for this or any of the other AF-P lenses.

    The AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G ED (and ED VR) are relatively lightweight lenses with a greater than 4:1 zoom range. But it's too bad that the lower end of the zoom range doesn't extend to the 55 mm of the AF-P 18-55mm lens. It's all plastic construction is fine except that for a long lens like this, the bayonet mount should probably have been made of metal for robustness. Even the bayonet mount on the much shorter 18-55mm lenses gets destroyed quite frequently. This one is more prone to such failures. Replacements are available.

    This is 1 of only 3 types of AF-P DX lenses using the so-called "pulse" stepper motor focusing system. The most common is the AF-P DX 18-55mm f/1:3.5-5.6G VR "kit" lens. The third is the AF-P DX 10-20mm f/1:4.5-5.6G VR wide angle lens. There would probably have been more but Nikon was transitioning over to the mirrorless "Z" cameras at around the same time the AF-Ps came out. Z lenses use similar stepper motor technology for focusing.

    Both VR and non-VR versions of the AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G Zoom Lens are available, which are believed to have virtually identical construction, so most of what follows should apply to both. (The AF-P FX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3E ED VR lens is significantly different even aside from the required larger optics) with among other things metal a Bayonet Mount and electronic aperture control with no aperture lever - thus the "E" after the f numbers. (Ask Google what the "G" in the other lenses stands for. It's... amusing.) The latter is also consistent with the transition to Z gear being what that uses but it is supposed to be compatible with late model DSLRs.)

    A partial dissection of the non-VR version may be found at: Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G ED Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album.

    Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G VR lenses:

    • ID #1 (SN: 21019592): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition and appears to be fully functional, AF and VR tested...

    • ID #2 (SN: 20076766): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition except for "click" when adjusting Zoom in the vicinity of 210 mm. But appears to be fully functional, AF and VR tested...

    • ID #3 (SN: 20966522): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition except for busted Bayonet Mount. The Bayonet Mount / Back Shell from Non-VR ID #1 was swapped. The annoyance is that it will not have "VR" displayed on the label. Well, one can't have everything. Swapping the labels might be possible but the results cosmetically could be a disaster. This also means it has the SN from non-VR #1...

    • ID #4 (SN: 20552214): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition, perfect functional condition, VR tested...

    • ID #5 (SN: 20390659): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition, perfect functional condition, VR tested...

    • ID #6 (SN: 21019633): Near perfect cosmetic condition in box, perfect functional condition. AF tested, VR pending but makes the correct noise. ;-)

    • ID #7 (SN: 20224278): Near perfect cosmetic condition, perfect functional condition. AF and VR tested...

    • ID #8 (SN: 20661562): Very good condition except for broken Bayonet Mount. Some spots behind front lens cluster. Swapped Backshell with non-VR #2, transferred labels, and Rubber Grip just because this one was a bit shiny. Cleaned inside Front Lens Cluster..

    Non-VR Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-6.3G ED lenses:

    • ID #1 (SN: 20914467): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition. But the focus assembly has been displaced or is broken. This lens is used for the photos in the dissection, above. Damaged Back Shell / Bayonet Mount from VR #3 swapped (SN: 20966522) and is not secure.

    • ID #2 (SN: 20780675): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition but appears dead except for aperture readout, meaning AF doesn't respond.

    • ID #3 (SN: 20688249): Very good cosmetic condition except for broken mount. AF tests OK.

    General comments:

    • The Bayonet Mount and Rear Shell are a single molded plastic piece just like the AF-P DX 18-55mm lens. It may be replaced without detaching any cables. This is not true of most AF-S lenses.

    • The Camera Contacts are held in place by the Rear Cover Ring without their own assembly. So they all fall out when disassembling the back of the lens. This is true of all the AF-P lenses. As long as that is expected, it isn't really a problem.

    • Replacing the Front (1st) Lens Cluster or Rear Lens Cluster is straightforward without extensive disassembly. However, whether each is truly interchangeable between samples of the lens is not known. But there were no shims or spacers on the sample lens. Going deeper is more involved and appears to be a major undertaking. That would be required to repair the focus mechanism. Stay tuned.

    The specific lens in the dissection, above, shows no signs of trauma. However, the focus optics are way off-center. Focus only works even remotely correctly over a narrow range of Zoom, and only clicks otherwise. The resulting photos even if the camera thinks it has focus lock are dreadful, Something in the focus mechanism has broken and/or popped out of place resulting in one or more lens elements being shifted and/or tilted. If this were a VR lens, I would blame that, it isn't. This could conceivably be a simple repair, but so far, even after taking the lens largely to bits, the focus assembly is still not accessible. Stay tuned.

    The conclusion so far though is that if there is any problem with the focus mechanism, get a replacement lens. :-( It is buried so deeply inside that the entire thing needs to be disassembled to get to it, let alone to fix the problem. Focus issues in the equivalent AF-S DX lens are trivial in comparison. And NO detailed information could be found on-line with or without the assistance of AI. This assessment may change if I find a totally smashed sample to disassemble. It may turn out that there is a shortcut to getting to the focus assembly. There are a pair of small screws and pegs which I orginally thought had to be removed to remove the Zoom Barrel and related parts. But they may be securing the focus assembly. ;-) Interestingly, after partially disassembling the lens and then putting back together, the focus assembly offset seems to be smaller. Go figure.

    For many other problems, access is straightforward. The simplest repair (which is very common) is to install a replacement Bayonet Mount / Rear Shell. They are available for around $25 but the replacement will not have the Aperture lever assembly, so that would need to be transferred. If swapped from a similar lens, then it's even simpler. It's the same procedure as repairing the AF-P DX 18-55mm lenses - a bit fiddly but all screwdriver work and can be done without even unplugging any ribbon cables. See the section: Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 18-55mm f/3.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens Bayonet Mount Transplant.

    Nikon AF-P FX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-5.6E ED VR Zoom Lens

    There appear to be no on-line repair manuals for this or any of the other AF-P lenses.

    The AF-P FX 70-300mm lens is the only full frame AF-P lens. There isn't even a "standard" or "Kit" AF-P FX lens. The assumption is that Nikon terminated designs for new AF-P lenses to concentrate on Z lenses, which share some of the same DNA. But interestingly, there is no 70-300mm Z lens either. This lens may also be the first one to have an electronically-controlled aperture (thus the "E" after the f/ numbers). With the aperture lever gone, there is no longer any real way to manually open the aperture to inspect inside the lens if it isn't already wide open. (However, the default should be wide open, so this should usually be irrelevant unless the lens or camera is faulty.) This is also the only AF-P lens to have physical switches for focus (A/, M/A, M) and VR (Off, Normal, Sport). There is an AF-S FX 70-300mm f/1:3.5-5.6G VR lens but its optical architecture and construction differ beyond the SWM versus stepper motor focusing. However, this lens probably has more in common with the AF-S version than with the other AF-P lenses. These include the camera contacts having their own housing with flex cable, the physical switches for AF and VR modes, and the metal bayonet mount with separate plastic backshell.

    While largely of plastic construction, this lens is HEAVY - 24 ounces or 1.5 pounds (680 g). Most of that weight is probably the glass optics. The Zoom guide is machined aluminum but its weight is probably small. Although slightly larger than the DX version, they look generally similar except for the location of the Focus Ring and the metal Bayonet Mount for the FX version. And sellers on eBay sometimes confuse them. Note that there is little point in acquiring this FX lens if you don't have a full-frame camera or expect to upgrade to one. The DX version is just as good and typically less than half the price.

    And having disassembled a sample down to the level needed to repair the most common problem not caused by a trauma - a crack in the Aperture/Focus Flex Cable - there is little point in acquiring one of these if it not functional unless you are interested in a challenging adventure. And also not if you've never been inside a modern lens before. And probably even if you have. The cost to send it out for repair would easily exceed that of a guaranteed working used lens. And while the repair is straightforward, replacing and securing the flex cable where it needs to go to avoid the same thing happening again quickly could be a challenge. In a nutshell, the replacement cable needs to be secured with its adhesive in exactly the right spot while in essense working blindfolded with one hand tied behind your back. ;( ;-) OK, it's not quite that bad, only blindfolded in one eye. ;-) Much more below and to come. I have ordered replacement flex cables.

    Nikkor AF-P FX 70-300mm f/3.5-5.6E ED VR Lenses:

    These are probably the only samples I will ever have as I do not have and do not not expect to acquire any full-frame F-mount cameras! Unless really good deals present themselves. ;-) Or, if focus repair is found to be not too fiddly; Then broken ones would be considered at the right price. But that appears to NOT be the case. More below.

    • ID #1 (SN: 2011405): Excellent to perfect cosmetic condition as though it has never been used, but focus only works over part of the Zoom and/or Focus range and it is not totally consistent. This was initially thought to be a mechanical problem, which is common with lenses like the AF-S DX 18-200mm. But a Web search seems to point to either a bad ribbon cable (makes sense) or focus motor (not so much). Unfortunately, the optimal focus position varies by both the Zoom setting and subject distance, so it's a crap shoot as to whether there will be focus lock for any given shooting situation. When it works, it works well, for what that's worth. The only Zoom setting where it seems to be fairly consistent is near 300mm, where it will usually focus from 1.2m (spec'd minimum) to ∞. Focus issues appear to be relatively common with this model lens and may be more than it's worth in either labor or cost to remedy since like all the AF-P lenses, the focus mechanism is buried rather deep inside. There may also be aperture issues over some or all of the Zoom range where it works at all. A cracked ribbon cable would be consistent with both.

      Monitoring for electromagnetic emissions from the lens with a dozen turn coil underneath seems to indicate that there are no pulses being generated when Focus does not change, not that it is mechanically binding. But this is far from conclusive as it's conceivable that movement is needed to generates detectable EM pulses.

    • ID #2 (SN: 2010336): Near mint cosmetic condition and appears to be in perfect functional condition. Test of VR on the shaker stand still needs to be performed, but it makes the correct noise when VR is active...

    • ID #3 (SN: 2004591): Near mint cosmetic condition but focus is totally dead. VR may be OK based on the sound it makes.

    • ID #4 (SN: 2035482): Very well worn cosmetic condition (99 percent of the gold lettering is worn off as is the silver Nikon on the lens cap), but there is no obvious physical damage and the glass is in excellent to pristine condition. However, Focus (A and M) and Aperture control are dead. The camera recognizes the lens (after a several second delay presumably attempting to initialize focus) and displays the maximum aperture as a function of Zoom setting, but does not focus or adjust the aperture based on Manual settings. In Manual mode the camera may also produce the dreaded "Error: Press shutter release button again" which can only be cleared by going to a different mode like Auto or removing the lens. (The cause of this is not entirely clear since the camera works perfectly with all other compatible lenses.) These symptoms are consistent with a bad internal flex cable. However, based on the sound the lens makes, VR appears to be functional. ;-) The rubber Zoom Grip is also a bit flabby. But most of that appears to be because it had been removed and replaced upside-down! Some pieces of tape that cover access holes under the Zoom Grip were also misplaced. So someone has been at least slightly investigating the failures, and based on other evidence, perhaps more than "slightly". ;-) But removing the Zoom Grip doesn't reveal much of anything by itself without going further. This is the lens used for the dissection.

    PCB connectors based on dissection, below:

    From top-left clockwise:

    • Camera Contacts. There are over twice as many connections in the flex cable as there are contacts, presumably to provide a lower impedance.

    • AF/VR Switches. Switch assembly on the Backshell.

    • AF Control. This also deals with the electronic aperture.

    • VR Control. Based on the process of elimination so far. ;-)

    • Manual Focus Encoder. A pair of opto-interupters physically in quadrature monitoring the teeth on the Focus Ring.

    • Zoom Position. A ~3.3K ohm slide pot forming a voltage divider fed from a low dropout voltage regulator like a TLV702 that tracks a slot in the zoom Barrel. This is a huge simplification compared to the digital encoders found on most or all AF-S lenses and even the AF-P DX 18-55mm "Kit" lens.

    • VR Gyros for Pitch and Yaw.

    Or see: Nikon AF-P Nikkor 70-300mm f/1:3.5-5.6E ED VR Zoom Lens Main PCB Flex Cables.

    Errors are possible!

    Nikon AF-P FX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-5.6E ED VR Zoom Lens Focus/Aperture Cable Replacement

    CAUTION: While the replacement Focus/Aperture (and VR) cables are available from eBay and elsewhere, they may not come with all the adhesive areas required, so it will be essential to replicate the routing and attachment of the original cable including a fold. It is these that may be the ultimate stumbling blocks in achieving a successful and long-lived repair since reattaching the Flex Cable to parts of the lens can only be done once they are mostly inaccessible. The cable replacement requires almost total disassembly of the lens. Triple color-coded match-marks must be added with the lens in a known Zoom setting (70mm unless not possible) before each subassembly is removed to achieve correct orientation when put back together. They may appear to have 3-fold 120-degree symmetry but most do not. All parts and their associated screws must be stored in separate labeled baggies as there are multiple screw types. Some screws should get a thread-locker when reinstalled. And a few have narrow shallow flat heads which are easily mangled, so a custom-modified screwdriver may be required. The Zoom position sensor also requires adhesive to help the single screw, which is very wimpy. There may be others. There may be upgraded reinforced and/or slighly redesigned flex cables available that should be installed in place of the originals. However, if such things exist at all, they are likely not as readily available and are certainly are not what is found on eBay.

    Unless you enjoy this sort of stuff, buying a used replacement lens would save lots of frustration. And if your time is worth anything above $0/hour, it will save money as well. ;-) Figure several hours overall if everything goes perfectly - and that no tiny parts pop off into oblivion. It may be that failure of the Focus Flex Cable is common with this model lens because even the factory assembly procedures are not perfect and/or difficult to perform properly, amd/or that the design is fundamentally flawed. So even if done perfectly, it may not last. Having said all that....

    And there was a crack forming on the VR Flex Cable of lens #4 as well in nearly the same spot as on the Focus/Aperture Flex Cable, so this appears to be a systemic issue with this model lens. Whether higher serial number samples have addressed it is not known but Google doesn't think so. Once a design has been released, I have am not aware of Nikon revising it or issuing a recall unless it would really hit their bottom line. Regardless, it would make sense to replace both cables at the same time. The effort would be nearly identical. Or a better solution may be to simply punt as noted and buy a replacement. At least then you have a bunch of spare parts like the front and back glass and plastic housings which are relatively easily replaced. :( ;-)

    Here is the detailed procedure needed to access the Focus/Aperture Flex Cable where cracks develop of the conductors, and is responsible for the most common flakey behevior associated with this lens. Almost all these steps also apply to replacement of the VR Flex Cable. They live in similar conditions with the same flexing taking place when zooming in and out. And based on one (1) sample point, it is likely to fail not far behind the Focus/Aperture Flex Cable. Since most of work is identical for both and new cables do not cost that much, it may be prudent to replace them both at the same time. With proper care, the disassembly and reassembly steps are not that complicated, but securing and routing the replacement cables in situ may be a major hassle, along with the risk of collateral damage to the other small flex cables.

    The only tools required are:

    1. Tiny JIS (similar to Philips) screwdriver. But the 00 Philips bit in a Husky™ combination Philips/flat screwdriver set fits them perfectly.

    2. Thin small flat blade screwdriver. A standard 1/8" or so blade may need to be ground down to fit the thin slots. This is critical to avoid chewing them up (or worse).

    3. Low power soldering iron to disconnect the Grounding wire from the Grounding Ring to the Main PCB. It is only needed for a fraction of a second.

    4. And a low power (e.g., 3x-5x) magnifier or loupe.

    Since there is no repair manual available for this (or any AF-P) lens, I have taken the liberty of making up names for some of the parts. At least they should be reasonably consistent with those in the dissection, below. More or less. ;-)

    Disassembly

    The first set of steps is quite straightforward and with reasonable care is quick and reversible. But storing each set of parts (e.g., the Bayonet Mount, Grounding Ring, and associated screws) in a clean dry sealable plastic bag and then labeling it is essential to assuring that the various screws go back where they belong. There are multiple sizes in both diameter, thread type, and length. Otherwise there is a real risk of stripping threads or causing other damage where the screws don't match the holes.

    Referring to the Dissection photos in the lik below may be of value here.

    Back section paarts:

    1. Remove the 4 tiny screws securing the Rear Cover Ring from the side of the Bayonet Mount. The Rear Cover Ring will come free and can be set aside with the tiny screws.

    2. Set Zoom to 100mm or higher and pop the Camera Contacts Housing away from the Bayonet Mount toward the center of the lens. Remove the 4 screws securing the Bayonet Mount and remove it. Pop the Grounding Ring away from the BackShell and unsolder the Grounding wire. Set it aside along with the Bayoner Mount and screws. Take care not to lose the rubber gasket surrounding the Bayonet Mount.

    3. Carefully remove the Backshell by sliding it away from the lens taking care not to stress the AF/VR Switch Assembly flex cable. Unplug it from the Main PCB. There are no latches on any of the flex cable connectors.

    4. Unplug the Camera Contacts flex cable from the Main PCB and set it aside.

    5. Carefully unplug the 3 flex cables from the sides of the Main PCB.

    6. Remove the single screw securing the Main PCB and unplug the remaining flex cables from the bottom. Store the Main PCB aside in an antistatic bag, along with the screw in a separate bag inside it.

    7. Slip off the Manual Focus Ring.

    8. With Zoom at 70mm, remove the Rubber Zoom Grip by lifting an edge and pulling it free from the front of the lens to avoid damage to the exposed parts in the back. Work around it pulling in small increments. The rubber is pretty tough. ;-)

    9. Peel off the 2 tape/plastic covers from the Zoom Barrel at around the 120mm position and one third of the way to the right of it. These have silver sheets and pieces of tape securing them. The black squarea and long strip can stay for now.

    Up to this point, everything is easily replaced in reverse order with minimal risk of daamge as long as care is taken to avoid tearing any flex cables. The trickiest part perhaps being resoldering the Ground wire. ;-) Except for the Bayonet mount, everything goes in an obvious location or orientation.

    Some of the following steps will remove parts that can appear go back oriented &pllusmn;120 degrees from what is correct. Some or all have slight offsets from exactly 120 degrees offsets, which would actually make it a bit less arbitrary since all three screws or pegs would fit only for one orientation. Regardless, these are more fiddly, but probably not as fiddly as some later ones. ;( ;-) Therefore, before any major parts are removed, match marks should be added with Zoom set at 70mm. In principle, 1 is sufficent but as seen in the dissection photos, technicolor RGB marks were add in the dissection to be safe. Note that there probably is at least 1 Nikon-certified set of match marks present, but probably white and boring. ;-)

    CAUTION: Do not put match marks on sliding surfaces if the marker uses something like paint that can scrape off and add debris inside the lens.

    1. Remove the 3 screws securing the Zoom Housing Assembly to the Fixed Core. (This is the structure to which all other parts of the lens are ultimately attached, directly or indirectly via other structures including the VR Gyros and Bayonet Mount.) The screws are visible in the dissection photos with: "Nikkor-AF-P-70-300mm_F4.5-5_6E_ED_VR_Back_Side_No_Manual_Focus_Ring" in the title. The heads are on the ring halfway up the stepped cylinder with the screw heads facing the back of the lens.

    2. Put match marks on the Zoom Barrel Assembly and Fixed Core. Aligning these with the white dot that identifies the top of the lens when mounted on the camera is as good a place as any.

    3. Slide the Zoom Barrel Assembly off of the Fixed Core taking care not to tear the Manual Focus Encoder flex cable, which is the only one that will come free with this. The thin part of the flex cable that may be stuck using adhesive that needs to be carefully peeled off.

    4. Adjust Zoom to the 70mm position (minimal length).

    5. Using a *matching* flat head screwdriver, remove each of the 3 screws locking the Front Lens Cluster Barrel to the Fixed Core one at a time. After each screw has been removed, slide the two sections (the Fixed Core and 1st Lens Cluster Barrel slightly apart, to a higher Zoom setting) and carefully remove the Zoom Guide (nut and roller). The sequence for one of them is shown in the dissection photo: "Nikkor-AF-P-70-300mm_F4.5-5_6E_ED_VR_Remove_Front_Lens_Cluster_Barrel_Guide_Post1". I don't think they can actually fall through the slots to inside but care is still required. ;-)

      The screws in the lens from which this disassembly procedure and the dissection exhibited definite abuse of the heads. The counter-clockwise damage would appear to indicate that someone was in here before me, though there is no other evidence of that. These screws (and probably those that secure the other guides) have a thread-locker since they have metal-to-metal thread contact, so initial removel would be challenging. A thread-locking compound should be used upon re-assemlby.

    6. Slide the 1st Lens Cluster Barrel fully out and off the Fixed Core. This will reveal the Zoom Programmer - the aluminum cylinder witih milled angled / helicoidal slots that control how the 3 movable lens clusters move when changing the Zoom setting. (The 2nd from the front is fixed. The 3rd and 4th move together maintaining the same relative position. Why they have separate tracks in the Zoom Programmer (or close enough for government work) is not clear as they appear to be totally parallel, but perhaps so that they can be removed individually).

    Nikon AF-P FX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-5.6E ED VR Zoom Lens Dissection

    See the start: Nikon AF-P FX Nikkor 70-300mm f/4.5-5.6E ED VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album.

    The subject is lens #4, above. Except for "Stock" and the next 4 "Angled" views, all photos are of that actual lens and its organs in various stages of deconstruction. The dissection goes down slightly beyond the level required to replace either the Focus (including Aperture control) Flex Cable or the VR Flex Cable. Some assemblies like the "Front (1st) Lens Cluster Barrel" were disassembled just for completeness since there is no need for that when replacing anything else. ;-) And bottom level assemblies like VR, Aperture, and Focus were left intact since for this lens at least, they are unlikely to be faulty. (That's not the case for the non-VR AF-P DX 70-300mm lens #1 with a mechanically screwed up focus mechanism, details TBD!).

    As can be seens in some of the dissetion photos, there is a major crack of the conductors across the entire width of the Focus/Aperture cable. And a crack is forming on the VR cable as well. In part due to the latter (and some screwups) as well as the fact that it isn't clear how to route and secure the replacement cables to minimize the chance of repeat trouble, revival of this lens is not going to occur. If it had been successful, #1 and #3 would have followed.

    Optimistically, the entire dissection is probably under 1 hour start to finish including bagging all the sets of parts and adding match marks to all the parts with quasi 3-fold symmetry. Somewhat less if done a dozen times. ;-)

    Description and more coming soon.

    Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 10-20mm f/4.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens

    There appear to be no on-line repair manuals for this or any of the other AF-P lenses.

    This is a nice light-weight wide angle lens. 10-20 mm is equivalent to 15-30 mm for the FX and old 35 mm film format. It slightly overlaps the range of the 18-55mm "kit" lens at the high end. As noted in the previous section, this is 1 of only 3 AF-P DX lenses with different focal length ranges currently - and probably forever.

    And a note: This is the only lens I've come across so far where parts like the Filter Ring and Front Lens Cluster can be removed and replaced without peeling off and distressing any labels (or at least not by much!). Both the outer label with the printing and the inner one that only serves as a bezel can be left in place. Use a grippy rubber "tool" like a piece of bicycle inner tube or something sticky like masking tape to grab the area of the outer label at two diametrically opposite locations near the edge and rotate it counter-clockwise. That's it for the Front Lens Cluster: The entire thing simply unscrews!. Then the Filter Ring can be taken off by removing 4 screws. (There are three notches in the mounting ring for the Front Lens Cluster, hidden by the inner blank label. Those no doubt would be used by Nikon.) It's possible that there is supposed to be some type of adhesive or thread locker to prevent the Front Lens Cluster (in particular) from loosening on its own, but there was no evidence of any on the 2 lenses that were disassembled. When tight, the "NIKKOR 10-20mm VR" on the label should line up with the top (index mark) of the lens. However, this may not be true if swapping parts between lenses particularly if the Spacer thickness differs, since that label would be the last thing to be added after final assembly. Thus there may be one or more Spacers behind the Front Lens Cluster thickness-coded by color or material. These should stay with the specific Front Lens Cluster. That collection of 4 lens elements - two that are highly curved - may differ by a fraction of 1 mm in effective focal length from one sample to the next and the Spacers compensate for that. It's also a good idea to mark the Spacers as they are removed as to orientation and top/bottom since they can go back multiple ways but only one will allow the Filter Ring mounting screws to pass through and reach their holes. If the Spacers aren't the correct thickness, near or far focus (at the very least) may not be achievable.

    And in the "Well, that's interesting department": With the Front Lens Cluster removed entirely, optimal focus is just beyond the Filter Ring and it will take closeup photos. Approximately 1.5 and 0.75 inches fill the width of the DX frame with Zoom at 10mm and 20mm, respectively. At 10mm, there is a fair amount of pincushion distortion but at 20mm distortion is minimal. And any decent photo editing program should be capable of compensating. ;-)

    Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 10-20mm f/4.5-5.6G VR lenses:

    • ID #1 (SN: 261895): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition and fully functional, AF and VR tested.

    • ID #2 (SN: 223836): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition and fully functional, AF and VR tested...

    • ID #3 (SN: 296490): Well worn (shiny plastic) but still in decent cosmetic condition with no significant scratches or dings though there is peeling ink on the label. However, the rubber zoom grip doesn't seem at home on this lens as it is very slightly too large length-wise. Perhaps it is a mis-matched transplant (though I cannot determine from what lens) or one from the Far East that doesn't quite fit. There are no real signs of deterioration as it is still supple and resilient, not flabby or stretchy indicating rubber decay. Soaking the rubber grip in boiling water for a half an hour made no difference. ;-)

      But functionally, the lens is a disaster. While it is recognized by the camera, focus does nothing (both AF and M) and there is a grinding noise when the camera is powered on attributed to failed VR initialization, and delays Live View or shutter response until it settles down. There is also a very slight roughness of the Zoom Ring near the 20mm position. The EXIF data does appear to show that the approximate Zoom setting (based on the encoder in the lens) is being sent to the camera. My guess before going inside was that there is a damaged or loose cable. However, after removing the parts from the back of the lens (Cover Ring, Bayonet Mount, and Back Shell), there is no obvious evidence of trauma to any cables, connectors, or the PCB. And after reseating cables and expanding the 8 Camera Contacts so they make, uh, better contact, there was no change in behavior upon reassembly.

      A couple days later on a whim - having not done anything with the lens other than to fondle it a bit ;-) - I stuck the lens back on a D5600. And focus worked perfectly (both AF and M) and even VR appeared to be functional on my test stand. (The stuttering at power-on from VR is sometimes present but that is infrequent and didn't take place on the test stand.)

      Then it stopped working - and started working again - and stopped - and started. ;-) A classic intermittent, though it doesn't seem to correlate with anything; Focus is either fully functional or totally dead after the camera is turned on. If it works, it appears to be reliable. So perhaps it's an initialization issue. The obvious solution then is to never turn the camera off! ;-) But it seems to be becoming more reliable as time goes on. It just sometimes needs a few power cycles to cooperate.

      Now it appears as though rotating Zoom from end-to-end while it's clicking will usually allow it to initialize properly. Go figure. ;-)

    • ID #4 (SN: 352789): Very good to excellent cosmetic condition and fully functional except VR needs to be tested.

    • ID #5 (SN: 375891): Cracked filter ring, should be otherwise OK. Swapped with filter ring and front lens group from #4. AF and VR tested...

    A Web Album with some views of ID #2 may be found at Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 10-20mm f/4.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens Web Album.

    And a Web Album with the early stages of the dissection of ID #3 may be found at Nikon AF-P DX Nikkor 10-20mm f/4.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album.

    More to come, perhaps.

    Nikon AF-S FX Nikkor 28-300mm f/3.5-5.6G ED VR Zoom Lens

    There is a repair manual on-line for this lens. Search for "Nikon AF-S Nikkor 28-300mm f3.5-5.6G ED Lens Factory Service Manual" (without the quotes). It's the first hit using Google. Though now it seams that the Learn Camera Repair Website is charging a small amount for these manuals. It is worth the cost just for the information value even if you have no intention of disassembling the lens. But if you're a cheapskate, it's still free at ManualsLIB.com (as of April 2025).

    This a full frame (FX, 35 mm) lens so it really has no business being here. ;-) Actually, it does. An FX lens will work fine with a DX camera. It's just a bit of a waste since ~half the picture area is outside the DX frame. The main reason it IS here is that its construction is believed to be very similar to that of the DX 18-200mm and 18-300mm lenses AND one was found on eBay just over $125 that is optically in very good condition but certifiably very broken mechanically - something is loose/rattling around inside including aperture parts which hvae been mangled, and the aperture is always wide open (but partially blocked by the parts floating around inside the lens!), Zoom gets stuck with serious dragging and grinding and doesn't go out beyond ~32 mm (never to 28 mm), focus is temperamental, VR seems dead, and there may be a motorized ratcheting noise when first powered. Aside from those minor issues, the lens is in perfect operating condition. ;-) And it does take decent photos when it feels like it (if you don't mind the aperture being more or less wide open). ;-) In any case, mostly complete dissection has been performed - as far as necessary until the sources of the problems are located - which is down to the level of removing the Aperture and VR assemblies. Which is to say, a nearly a total disassembly.

    See the Nikon AF-S FX Nikkor 28-300mm f/3.5-5.6G VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album. (The first few are all either Nikon stock photos or those from the KenRockWell.com Website (which appear to include some Nikon stock photos!), then the lens architecture diagram, after that are all of the distressed lens itself. Those photos were taken with a Nikon D90 using a Nikkor AF-S DX 18-55G VR II lens on a tripod without VR (it's dead on that lens anyhow) and remote shutter release. Most are illuminated by a ~16x16 inch LED fixture with a uniform diffuser ~16 inches above the shooting area. The camera's White Balance is set to Fluorescent Cool White and minor picture adjustments are done in post processing. The background is a single sheet of glossy white paper. There has been no background removal partially because those programs that generate images with decent resolution are no longer free as they were for the earlier dissections, or are now too cumbersome to use with multiple photos. But with the white background for most, there is little need for it. Those without a white background were done that way to enhance the appearance as needed.

    The first block of photos after the lens architecture diagram show the intact lens. The shiny complexion is not an artifact of lighting, it definitely appears that way having seen lots of use, but no actual physical trauma is evident. The broken aperture actuator plate or a piece of it is visible in the view through the front of the lens. ;-)

    The dissection will be guided by the disassembly procedure in the repair manual, skipping those steps that are irrelevant. A substitute for at least one special tool may need to be improvised. While a reasonable effort will be made to avoid unnecessary destruction, some damage will almost certainly occur. But the aperture mechanism is already history and there has likely been irreversible havoc caused by the crunching and grinding during Zoom and other scary sounds from Autofocus. Live with it. ;-)

    The next 15 or so photos after the lens architecture diagram are of the intact lens. Then the fun begins. The next block of photos are based on the disassembly procedure in the repair manual starting at the front of the lens. So, removal of the Filter Ring, 1st Lens Group, and 2nd Lens Group. The checkered yellow and white background is used for some photos of the lens group parts to give more depth to them. There is also a photo of the two pieces of the aperture mechanism that were floating around inside. The one on the left - the Aperture Actuator Plate - is missing the actuating tang and was originally totally folded in half! And the one on the right had to be partially folded to extract it through the hole left by the 2nd Lens Group. With those removed, Zoom is reasonably smooth and the crunching noises are gone. However, the aperture return spring is missing and Zoom still does not go below around 32 mm, possibly due the missing spring and/or tang being jammed in there somewhere. The missing aperture leaves probably wouldn't account for it.

    As the dissection has progressed further at the back of the lens, several mangled remains of the aperture leaves have fallen out. Three at first, then after removing the Main and SWM PCBs, three more along with a metal tang broken off the Shutter Actuating Plate, and the final three after the lens was disassembled down the Zoom Telescope Mechanism (my terminology). ;-) And the aperture return spring was found in-place with one end still attached by a dab of adhesive. That metal piece may have been what was interfering with Zoom because now the full range has been restored. If the lens was only used for shooting at the wide open 1:3.5-5.6 aperture (using aperture priority or manual control), it could now be reassembled in perfect condition. ;-) That's tempting but there is probably little demand for a lens that only works at maximum aperture. The aperture assembly for this lens doesn't appear to be something that appears on eBay! ;-( ;-)

    The dissection is now complete. The assembly that wasn't totally taken to bits is what the Repair Manual calls the "2nd lens-G straight ring unit". It's a pair of concentric cylinders with screws, guides, bearings, and springs which would like nothing else than popping off and getting lost. Otherwise, it's pretty much as described in the Repair Manual, though some steps were done out of order by accident or by design. As best as I can determine, nothing has been damaged so were an aperture assembly for this lens to appear on eBay at an affordable price I might consider attempt a restoration.

    One interesting observation is that the VR Gyro ICs (PN: XV-3500CB) are on the Main PCB and SWM PCB, rather than having a separate assembly of their own. The PCBs are mounted at right angles to each-other.

    Stay tuned.

    Nikon Z Lenses

    The Nikon Z cameras use a new series of lenses with a totally different mount. This was done in part because the lens-to-focal plane spacing of the Z cameras is much smaller, as well as due to electronics changes in the control of aperture, focus, VR, etc.

    The general scheme is the same for mounting and unmounting, but the Z mount is much larger in diameter, the ball contacts face forward in the camera instead of being in the lens facing inward, and there may be many more of them. Both FX and DX format lenses are automagically accomodated. Since Z lenses all use a pulse-type focus scheme similar to that of AF-P lenses, there is no direct support for the AF-S "Silent Wave Motor" (SWM) focusing.

    FTZ and FTZ-II F-to-Z adapters enable F-mount lenses to be used on Nikon Z cameras. They trick the Z camera microbrain into thinking there is a Z-mount lens attached to the adapter when it is actually an F-mount lens. This includes full electronic control of aperture and focus. But there are no optics inside, just the correct physical spacing to account for where the mirror mechanism would be in a DSLR. And motors and electronics to operate the aperture lever and focus mechanism. I am not aware of any adapter in the reverse direction. ;-)

    Nikon Z DX Nikkor 16-50mm f/4-5.6 VR Zoom Lens

    There is a repair manual for this lens available directly from the Nikon | Self Service Repair Website. Yes, there is now such a thing direct from Nikon no less which apparently is quite recent and only deals with Nikon Z gear. That is the good news. The bad news is that for this specific lens, it has virtually no useful information. In fact the number of pages devoted to notices, cautions, and warnings - most of which are totally irrelevant for a lens - outnumber those with repair information. However, some other repair manuals are much more comprehensive. So worth checking out in any case.

    The Nikkor Z DX 16-50mm f/3.5-5.6 VR lens is the most compact zoom lens for Nikon Z cameras. Its reviews are actually pretty good for a mostly plastic (except for the optics) lens. The optical architecture is simpler than other lenses with a similar zoom range, specifically the AF-P and AF-S DX 18-55mm "kit" lenses - only 7 optical elements versus 12 for the AF-P 18-55mm. Of course their minimum aperture is larger by an extra fraction of a stop. ;-) A comparison of these two lenses is shown in Nikkor Z DX 16-50mm f/1:4.5-6.3 VR Z vs AF-P Comparison1. While the zoom range for the AF-P is also wider by around 10 percent, these don't account for its dramatically larger size. But this diminutive lens does look a bit silly on a massive Z SLR, sort of like an ancient Kodak. ;-)

    The Z 16-50mm is also the least expensive Nikon Z zoom lens and broken samples are available at relatively decent prices - which is perfect for evaluation and dissection. ;-) In fact, it may be the ONLY affordable sacrificial Nikkor Z lens likely to show up on eBay more frequently than total solar eclipses. Anything Nikon Z-related seems to be at least 4 times the cost of Nikon F gear. And many other Nikkor Z lenses approach $1,000 and beyond, even used.

    Note that when mated to a full-frame (FX) camera, the coverage for this lens is DX: AFS-C or around 1x0.6 inches. The Z full frame cameras know that and will crop the photos auto-magically. So except for giant blowups, that may not even be noticed.

    I have two samples of this lens:

    • ID #1 (SN: 20141340): This was described as having "front end damage". I wonder if their collision insurance covered it. ;-) And indeed, the Zoom Barrel was jammed at an angle and split along its entire length with the sections shifted with respect to each-other by a several mm. In fact, the entire front section of the lens was so cockeyed that zoom would not move at all. However, some gentle persuasion remedied that so the lens works mechanically even with the split Zoom Barrel. But the front Lens Cluster was also pushed in on one side by a couple of mm with trauma to the label evident. This results in it taking truly lousy picture with focus varying from side-to-side across the field of view.

      After removing the label, the problem could be easily seen: Two of the three plastic supports for the 1st Lens Cluster were totally fractured. Repairing these - at least to a state where it takes decent, if not perfect, photos - was quite straightforward. One of the fractured supports was reattached with 5-Minute Epoxy. Actual plastic cement would probably be better but you use what you have. ;-) The third was too small to be readily positioned while the glue set and held in place by itself, so it was attached to the 1st Lens Cluster assembly with its normal screw and a drop of 5-Minute Epoxy was added. Then the entire unit was installed using the other two supports. It is hopefully in close enough alignment for government work. ;-) The cracked Zoom Barrel (visible in some of the dissection photos) does not really seem to cause any mechanical or optical problems. This is almost certainly not a perfect or rugged repair, rather more of a quick and dirty "proof of concept". But as they say, if it works, use it. And for now it seems to be working. ;-) And it has been used to take some of the later dissection photos.

      However, this does cause a different sort of problem in that since this lens now works more-or-less, its CPU PCB will not be available to repair #2 if required. That, however, turned out to not really be viable regardless.

    • ID #2 (SN: 20319727): The description said "Does not initialize" which might indicate a broken flex cable, other bad connections, or a bad CPU. However, that is not the case. Zoom is very rough and will not reach the collapsed position. Sometimes the lens will initialize but then autofocus doesn't work and the manual focus ring has no effect, though the focus stepper motor can be heard attempting to do its thing. And indeed there is an optic loose in the back of the lens which may be the Middle Lens Cluster group as its position affects focus. If the camera is pointed at the ceiling and the image will come into reasonably sharp focus. My guess is that this lens was also in an accident and was slammed into a hard surface, front or back, but with no visible damage to the exterior. That would be like the car engine breaking off its mounts but with no external evidence of a collision. Explain that to your insurance company. ;-)

      However, once the Back Lens Cluster was removed, the something literally fell out - a piece of black plastic, though its origin is not yet clear. However, a problem was found after nearly completely disassembling the lens. A slightly mangled spring that provided the restoring force for the focus group did literally fall out. It was supposed to be stretched between a pair of tiny plastic tabs, one on the Focus Lens Group and the other on the Middle Lens Cluster housing. One of those tabs had broken off. Whether that was the original cause or collateral damage is not clear. More to come on that specifically. The solution to that at least was to simply extend the end of the spring that went to missing tab to a handy cross-wise bar nearby. It isn't quite up to Nikon standards but should work. Now if I can only get the thing back together. For this lens, most of the cylindrical parts have 3-fold symmetry except for the orientation of the front label. This it NOT necessarily true of other Nikon lenses. Which is why match marks should always be added if there aren't detailed instructions - it probably has some. However, I did not expect it to be repairable

      But alas, the popped spring was only the tip of the proverbial iceberg. Two of three plastic tabs that rotate the Zoom Barrel with broken off and the third was on its way there. Other bits of plastic were found lodged inside. And with the actual cause unknown, it is unlikely that this lens will ever be completely reassembled. It's one of those situations where something wasn't quite right resulting in a cascade of failures. My guess is that once the spring tab broke, the spring and/or tab piece jammed the lens, and then someone forceably attempted to change zoom breaking those other tabs.

      It's also possible that this is a common problem with these lenses whereby forcing zoom either beyond the stops or due to stiffness from inadequate or gummed up lubrication breaks or bends those tabs. And it's all downhill from there. I have seen at least two other Z DX 16-50mm lenses on eBay with stuck zoom that appear pristine in every other respect.

      The final photos of the dissection show it back together and it now can be set from Collapsed to 50mm (is was jammed originally), But what's not obvious is that the Middle Lens Cluster with Lens Groups 4-6 (including Focus and VR) is not present. The lens might be recognized by a camera since the CPU PCB was reinstalled and attached to the zoom and focus encoder flex cables, but even if it was, the photos would be truly lousy. ;-)

      Since the front of the lens is undamaged, the Front Lens Cluster could be swapped into repaired #1 but that already performs well so there is little point. ;-) And replacement would require nearly total disassembly of both lenses, hardly justifiable if your time is worth anything.

    • ID #3 (SN: 20253184): This appears to be new but without the original packaging. And it works like new.

    Here is most of the dissection of ID #1: Nikon Z Nikkor DX 16-50mm 3.5-6.5 VR Zoom Lens Dissection Web Album.

    • The first half dozen or so photos show this lens in various poses as well as comparisons with the common (non-Z) AF-P DX 18-55mm f/1:3.5-5.6G VR "kit" lens, the Z 24-70mm f/1:4 S VR "kit" lens, and the Z 28-400mm f/1:4-8 VR "superzoom" lens, which has the highest zoom ratio of all the Z lenses.

Where the broad area illumination and/or the white background poor rendition of glass optics or reflections from metal shims or other surfaces like the CPU PCB, the checkered yellow and white tablecloth ;-) was used and/or a combination of fluorescent and LED lamps for illumination.
